A raw HID write macOS refuses, from the IOKit transport (`HidError` with a code) or the paired helper (`permission_denied` on macOS), leaves the flash waiting for the reset key with `instructionCode: "permission_input_monitoring"`. The dialog maps each code to **Open Files and Folders settings** or **Open Input Monitoring settings** through D-492's `POST /api/system/open-settings`, and shows its own words instead of the settings path. The flasher's sentences no longer say "your terminal"; the CLI prints where the setting is, once.
- **Cancel while waiting for the bootloader.** `POST /api/flashes/:id/cancel` aborts a flash only while it waits and before its first write in this process. Once the flasher has said it is writing, even for one half of a split board, the route answers `409 flash_writing` and the flash finishes, as `stop()` does for a sync (D-497). The Flash view carries `cancellable`, so the dialog offers **Cancel** only then. A cancelled flash is `errorCode: "cancelled"`: the dialog says what the flasher said ("Cancelled. Nothing was flashed.", plus the bootloader note when keyloom had restarted the keyboard) and offers **Flash to keyboard** again; the header goes back to "Ready to flash". This first version waited for Zapp to print before calling the flash `writing`. Zapp prints nothing while it writes, so the window was the whole write; D-506 replaces that.
- **Check the keyboard again.** `POST /api/flashes/:id/check` (and `keyloom flash --check <flash>`) runs keyboard sync's `readKeyboards` for a finished flash that isn't verified, on a board whose running build keyloom can read (`checkable`). It never builds, restarts or writes: a test runs it over fake USB, raw HID and serial and finds no write, no serial touch, no zapp run and no new build, flash or sync row. When the keyboard reports this build, the flash's log gains the verified line a flasher writes and the version is recorded, so the flash, the header and History agree. Otherwise it says what it saw (not connected, another build, still in its bootloader, other firmware) with Check again or **Flash again**. A board whose builds carry no marker says to type on it instead.
- **After review (with D-506):** the line after an Apply promises auto-sync or auto-apply only from the states it starts from: in sync, syncing, or waiting. An unplugged keyboard that auto-syncs is told it happens "when you connect it by USB"; one that needs a hand, failed, or runs a build keyloom doesn't know gets **Build and flash**, and a MacBook whose kanata isn't running gets **Apply now**. Once the sync view's head is the revision the page shows, the card follows it: "keyloom is applying it now", then "It is on your MacBook keyboard" with no button. **Check the keyboard again** answers `later_build` when every half runs a build this copy made after the flashed one, and offers no **Flash again**, which would roll it back. A failed flash's permission words lead the server's sentence instead of replacing it, so a split board's recovery result stays on screen. The revision question takes its answers from the board's revisions (`label` and `hint` in `board.json`), not from the Moonlander's.

## 2026-11-11: a stranded Corne half is flashed instead of refused

### D-504: A shared boot drive already open is written when every other half is on USB running firmware, and a copy that failed on open is never taken as done. Refines D-437 and D-465. (BK, 2026-11-11: "fix all the issues and ship", after a Corne flash stranded the right half)

- **What happened.** keyloom restarted the Keebart Corne's right half into its bootloader. Opening `firmware.uf2` on `KEEBART` failed with `EINTR`, and the drive unmounted. keyloom took the error plus the unmount as the bootloader's "written" (the ENXIO rule from [uf2-copy-enxio-taken-as-done](../issues/uf2-copy-enxio-taken-as-done.md)), waited 180 s for a half that was still in its bootloader, and failed. Flash again then refused the open `KEEBART` drive, because a shared label already mounted could be either half (D-437). The left half was on USB running its old build, so the drive could only be the right half; the flash was finished by copying the file by hand.
- **A failed open wrote nothing.** Node names the failing call in the error's `syscall`. When it is `open`, the drive going away is not the bootloader taking the file. keyloom waits up to 60 s for the drive to mount again, rebinds the mount path, identity and file path, and writes it, at most twice. A shared drive that comes back is not known to be the same half, since a peer entering its bootloader mounts the same label, so it is written only while every peer reports `running`, at the remount and at each later write. Only an `open` of the image write counts: the sync's open comes after a full write and keeps the ENXIO rule. If the drive does not return, the flash fails saying nothing was written. An `EINTR` on an open while the drive stays mounted is retried like `EACCES`. Errors after the open keep the ENXIO rule.
- **Positive proof attributes an open shared drive.** `restartHalf` reads every other half sharing the label. If each reports `running` (its keyloom or other firmware is on USB), the open drive can only be this half's, and the step proceeds as `alreadyThere` and `attributed`. A peer that is `absent` is not enough here, unlike D-465: a half in its bootloader shows no firmware on USB, so the open drive may be that very half. Recovery and normal flashing both accept an attributed drive, and each write to it re-reads the peers under the same strict rule; an unattributed one is refused as before.
- **A cancelled build's git locks are cleared, after its container.** Each west build container carries the label `dev.keyloom.checkout=<checkout>`. Holding the checkout's lock, a build removes any container with that label, which only a keyloom that died mid-build leaves, then the script deletes `*.lock` under each west project's `.git` before `west update` ([zmk-build-stale-git-lock](../issues/zmk-build-stale-git-lock.md)). A container doesn't depend on its client: with the client killed by `SIGKILL`, a `docker run --rm` container kept running, and the daemon removed it only once it exited (Docker 29.6.2 client, 28.4.0 engine, 2026-11-11).

## 2026-11-11 round 2: the MacBook remapper never strands anyone

### D-503: kanata's checklist follows an install, offers the update, and names the controls it points at. Follows D-493 (R2-06, R2-10, R2-11, R2-14). (Claude, from the round-2 dead-ends review)

- **Install kanata is a job the checklist follows.** `POST /api/system/remapper/install-kanata` starts `brew install kanata` and returns at once; the status read carries `install: { state, action, log, failure }`. While Homebrew runs, the step shows its last six lines and **Cancel install** (`POST …/install-kanata/cancel`); **Check again** and the Settings buttons stay usable. A failure keeps its reason and lines, offers **Try again**, and links **Download kanata** (the releases page), the way that doesn't need Homebrew. A cancel says so plainly and offers Install kanata again. The job is in `src/app/remapper-setup.ts` (`kanataInstaller`); it duplicates R2-A's install-job API for Docker and Zapp, and the two can merge later.
- **Cancel stops all of Homebrew.** `brew` runs Ruby, which runs curl; a SIGTERM to `brew` alone left its children holding the output open, so the install looked like it ran on (seen with a fake `brew` whose `sleep` outlived it). The kanata install spawns Homebrew in its own process group (`Bun.spawn({ detached: true })`) and signals the group, then SIGKILLs it 5 s later. The shared toolchain runner still signals only its own process; changing it touches every build and flash, so that is left for the merge with R2-A.
- **Update kanata.** The status read carries `kanata: { version, wanted, update }`. An older kanata that Homebrew installed (its real path is under `Cellar/kanata/`) gets **Update kanata** (`brew upgrade kanata`, through the same job, with **Cancel update**). Homebrew can't go back to an older version or update a kanata it didn't install, so a newer one, one from elsewhere, or an unreadable version links **Get kanata 1.12.0** (the release tag). The link also sits beside Update kanata. `brew upgrade` exits 0 with "already installed" when Homebrew has nothing newer, so after an update that leaves kanata older the read sends `update: "link"`, and the step says "Homebrew has nothing newer than 1.11.0" with the link and no second Update kanata. After an update the root copy differs, and the checklist offers **Set up and start kanata** (D-498).
- **Steps that matter while kanata runs.** The sync popover shows the checklist's update and set-up-again steps while kanata is running too, and nothing when there are none. The whole checklist still shows only while kanata isn't running.
- **"Auto-apply", where it is meant.** The MacBook's switch reads "Auto-apply is on/off/paused" (and Pause/Resume auto-apply), and the line that sends the person there names it: "The steps are under “Auto-apply is off” below." Other keyboards keep "Auto-sync".
- **Toasts with a button stay.** A toast with an action stays at least 12 s, and no toast leaves while the pointer is on it or focus is in it; after that it runs on with the time it had left, and never less than 2 s. The countdown starts once per toast, so a re-render no longer restarts it. A hidden page holds it too, and so does focus inside a toast when the window loses focus to another app. A full stack of three drops its oldest toast without a button first. `kanata_not_running` and `kanata_not_foreign` offer **Open keyboard status**, which opens the sync popover; `keyboard_removed` offers **Restore**, the same restore as Your keyboards, and says syncing stays paused until it is resumed.
- **Polish.** Putting a config or a version on the MacBook says keyloom loads it into kanata, not that it builds it. At 1440 × 900 the checklist popover scrolls inside itself (its menu's 60 % height limit) with no change here; the "Just plugged in" card that can cover it belongs to the keyboards screens.

### D-498: The LaunchDaemon runs a root-owned copy of kanata, and setup refuses a kanata that can run programs. Replaces D-497's "still open" note about Homebrew's kanata. (BK, 2026-11-11, asked "harden kanata's root daemon?", chose copying to a root-owned path; the config stays in the home folder.)

- **The copy.** The daemon ran `/opt/homebrew/bin/kanata`, which the person's account can replace, as root. Setup now copies kanata to `/Library/Application Support/keyloom/kanata` (root:wheel 755; the folder root:wheel 755) inside the same root script as the plist, and the plist runs that copy. Root copies into a `mktemp` file in its own folder and renames it into place only if its SHA-256 (`shasum -a 256`) is the one keyloom read before asking for the password; a kanata swapped while the dialog is open is refused and nothing is installed. The plist still travels base64-encoded in the script (D-497). The kanata binary is the one file root reads from a folder it doesn't own, and the hash pins it.
- **The config stays in the home folder.** Copying `keyloom.kbd` to a root-owned place would need a password on every Apply. So the kanata keyloom gives root must refuse configs that run programs.
- **Homebrew's kanata can't run programs.** kanata's `cmd` action is behind a Cargo feature. Homebrew's formula (`Formula/k/kanata.rb`, kanata 1.12.0) builds with `cargo install *std_cargo_args` and no `--features`, and kanata 1.12.0's default features are `tcp_server`, `win_sendinput_send_scancodes` and `zippychord` (`Cargo.toml`). Built without `cmd`, the parser refuses any `cmd` action with "cmd is not enabled for this kanata executable" (`parser/src/cfg/cmd.rs`) and logs "kanata was compiled to never allow cmd" (`parser/src/cfg/mod.rs`). BK's installed binary (`/opt/homebrew/Cellar/kanata/1.12.0/bin/kanata`, sha256 `385e5b05…7d0e`) contains that second string and not "DANGER! cmd action is enabled" (`strings`, 2026-11-11; the binary was not run).
- **Setup checks, not trusts.** Before asking for the password, setup runs `kanata --check` on a probe config (`danger-enable-cmd yes` and a `cmd` alias, the shape of kanata's own `parse_cmd` test). A kanata that accepts it is refused (`kanata_allows_cmd`: "This kanata can run programs from its config, so keyloom won't run it as root", with Download kanata); one that fails for another reason is refused too (`kanata_cmd_unknown`, with kanata's last line and Download kanata). The CLI prints no root command in either case and says why. Tested against stand-in kanata scripts for each answer; the probe has not been run against the real binary here, because this round runs no real kanata. `test/firmware/kanata/` already runs the real `kanata --check` where it is installed; a check of the probe against it would settle that.
- **The checklist says when to set up again, and only for a Homebrew update.** The doctor compares the kanata on PATH with the root copy by SHA-256. A daemon with no copy, set up before this decision, is `daemon_outdated` ("Set kanata up again"); BK's own Mac is in this case until he sets up again. A difference is `kanata_updated` ("kanata was updated. Set it up again", naming the version and path it copies) only when it looks like a Homebrew update: the real path is `Cellar/kanata/<v>/bin/kanata`, `--version` reports that `<v>` (a bottle rebuild's `_1` dropped), and the root copy reports another version. Both offer **Set up and start kanata** and need the root command again in `keyloom remap doctor`. Any other difference is `kanata_replaced` ("kanata changed outside Homebrew"): a process running as the person can write Homebrew's folder, and a replaced binary can print "cmd is not enabled" like the real one, so a changed hash alone must not lead to a trusted-looking password step. The step names the path and both versions and offers no password step for a Homebrew kanata; **Reinstall kanata** (`brew reinstall kanata`, the same job as Install) puts Homebrew's own back. A kanata from elsewhere has no Homebrew copy to put back, so it offers **Set up with this kanata** with "If you installed this kanata yourself". While kanata is stopped, Start kanata waits for that step. The doctor gives no root command for `kanata_replaced`; `keyloom remap setup` still prints one, because the person asks for it. This narrows the risk, not removes it: a process that can write Homebrew's folder can also fake a new Cellar folder and version, and the first setup trusts whatever kanata is on PATH.
- **A failed root script says why.** Each step prints `keyloom setup stopped (<code>): <reason>` to stderr before `exit 1`, so `sudo sh -c` shows the reason and osascript carries it in its "execution error" line. The app reads the code (`kanata_swapped`, `root_copy_failed`, `root_plist_failed`, `root_log_failed`, `root_start_failed`) and shows the reason; `kanata_swapped` says nothing was installed and to press the button again. Before, every failure showed "The command exited with a non-zero status. (1)".
- **One read of kanata.** Setup reads the binary once, hashes those bytes, and runs the cmd probe on a private copy of them (mode 700, in a `mkdtemp` folder), so the kanata probed is the one the hash pins. The real kanata 1.12.0 runs from that copy (`test/firmware/kanata/cmd-probe.test.ts`). The checklist's status read, which polls every 1.5 s during an update, no longer builds the root command it never shows.
- **One script for the app and the CLI.** `keyloom remap setup` prints the same script inside `sudo sh -c`, so the CLI no longer has root copy the plist from keyloom's folder either.
- **Privacy & Security.** The checklist now says the kanata to allow is keyloom's copy, in `/Library/Application Support/keyloom`. Not checked on hardware: whether macOS carries an Input Monitoring grant from Homebrew's kanata to the copy, or asks again after each set-up-again. The wording holds either way.
- **`include` can't leak a root-only file through the log.** kanata reads `keyloom.kbd` as root, and a config error prints the start of a file it `include`s. The daemon's log was `-rw-r--r--` root, so a process running as the person could write `(include /etc/master.passwd)` into `keyloom.kbd`, trigger a reload over TCP, and read the start of that file from the log. kanata's TCP replies carry no error text (`ReloadResult` is `{ ok }`, `tcp_protocol/src/lib.rs` @ v1.12.0), so the log is the channel; that a live reload error reaches it was not observed. The root script now creates `/var/log/keyloom-kanata.log` root:wheel 600 before it bootstraps the daemon (an older log becomes 600 and keeps its lines), and launchd appends to it without changing the mode. keyloom never reads this log; BK reads it with `sudo`.
- **keyloom never writes `include`.** The generator emits no `include` form. It did copy macro and tap-dance names into `;;` comments as they were, so a name with a line break (one the agent proposed, say) ended the comment and became a top-level `(include …)`. Comments now keep their text on one line. The apply path needs no `include` refusal of its own: it runs `kanata --check` as the person first, which can't read a root-only file, and refusing `include` in keyloom wouldn't stop a process that writes `keyloom.kbd` directly.
- **Still open.** A config kanata reads as root can do whatever a `cmd`-less kanata allows. Not checked: whether anything besides `cmd` and `include` lets a swapped config reach beyond remapping keys.

## 2026-11-11 round 2: Docker and tools never strand anyone

### D-500: An install can be cancelled, and the CLI runs the same install. Extends D-488 and D-489. (Claude, from the round-2 dead-ends review, R2-08, R2-13 and CLI parity)

- **Cancel.** `DELETE /api/system/install?tool=` aborts the install job's `AbortController`. The signal reaches `brew install` through `run()` and, for Docker, the Colima start through `startDockerEngine({ signal })` and the runner. The job stays `running` in phase `stopping` until Homebrew has exited, so a second Install click can't start a second `brew` beside the dying one. Homebrew runs in its own process group (`run({ processGroup: true })`), and Cancel signals the whole group: a `curl` that `brew` started would otherwise keep the output pipe open and hold the job in `stopping` until it finished. Then it is `cancelled`, which the page treats as no install: the not-installed view comes back with "You stopped the install." A cancel during Colima's first start leaves Docker installed, so the page offers **Start it now** (D-499).
- **Install log polling is quiet.** A missed poll no longer says "keyloom stopped answering while it installed". The server-gone banner says that once, for the whole page; the next poll carries on.
- **CLI parity (D-030).** `keyloom doctor --install-docker` runs `installTool("docker")`, the job the button runs, with Homebrew's lines on stderr, then the doctor checks. Ctrl-C reaches Homebrew through the same signal, because its own process group no longer gets the terminal's. It is a doctor flag, not a new `keyloom install` command, because it sits beside `--start-docker`: each fixes what the docker check reports, and the check runs after it. `--restart-docker` does the same for D-499's restart.
- **Shutdown.** Because Homebrew is outside the terminal's process group, `keyloom serve` cancels running installs when it stops, and serve and the doctor flags treat SIGHUP like Ctrl-C. A stop signals the group even after `brew` itself has exited, so a child still holding the output can't stall it in `stopping`.
- **`--install-docker` installs only where there is no Docker.** Beside Docker Desktop or OrbStack, a second `docker` in `/opt/homebrew/bin` could shadow the app's, so it says what is already there instead.
- **Rejected:** treating a cancelled install as `failed`. The person chose to stop, so a red "Docker didn't install" with Try again would describe a failure that didn't happen.

### D-499: Every Docker state ends at a button that fixes it. Extends D-487, D-488 and D-497. (Claude, from the round-2 dead-ends review, R2-02, R2-05, R2-07, R1-12 and R1-13)

- **A first start that fails after a good install** (`start_failed`) is no longer an install failure. The page drops back to readiness, which now says "Docker is installed, but Colima didn't start." with the reason and **Start it now**. Build is not blocked: a build starts the engine itself. Homebrew's `docker` stays on the `default` context until Colima's first successful start switches it, so on a Mac with no engine app `findDockerEngine` takes a `default` (or absent) context beside a `colima` on PATH as Colima's. Without that, readiness said keyloom didn't know the engine, and the page lost Start it now.
- **A failed install** keeps **Try again**, and adds **Download Docker Desktop** with OrbStack as a link (the readiness' `alternatives`). A failed Zapp or DFU install adds **Get {tool}**, the tool's own page.
- **`cli-missing`** names its app (`DockerStatus.app`). On a Mac the page offers **Open {app}**: `POST /api/system/docker/start` runs D-487's `open -a` path and waits while the status stays `cli-missing`, because the app's first run puts its CLI in place. keyloom now also looks for the CLI in the per-user folders those setups fill (`~/.docker/bin`, `~/.orbstack/bin`, `~/.rd/bin`). Build says "Install Docker's command line tools first." The OrbStack line no longer asks for `orb setup`. A build never opens the app by itself (`canStart` stays false). Podman Desktop brings no `docker` command, so its status names no app to open and says the command line tools are missing.
- **`no-access` from a Mac app** gets **Restart {app}**: `POST /api/system/docker/restart` quits it with `osascript -e 'quit application id "…"'`, waits until that application is not running, and opens it again on the D-487 path. The bundle ids are the ones each app's Homebrew cask quits (`com.docker.docker`, `dev.kdrag0n.MacVirt`, `io.rancherdesktop.app`, `io.podmandesktop.PodmanDesktop`). It restarts only a Docker that refuses this account, because quitting one that works stops whatever it runs, such as an auto-sync build. After the quit it always opens the app again, and keeps waiting while the socket still refuses or answers slowly; a refusal at the deadline says the restart didn't fix it. A Mac app runs as the person, so a fresh start gives the socket back (D-497). Colima keeps its copy-the-command fix, because that needs sudo. `doctor`'s no-access line is engine-aware: Colima's commands, the Linux group, or the app to restart.
- **An engine keyloom can't start** (`elsewhere`, `unknown`) keeps its own sentence and Check again, and Build says "Start your Docker engine first." A daemon that only answered slowly doesn't block Build, because a build waits for it.
- **Activity tests** stopped by Docker carry `reasonCode: "docker"` on the review evidence, and the review shows the release dialog's `DockerCallout` from that code, with **Test again** once Docker answers. The page never reads the sentence. A kanata board shows no activity tests, in the review or the new-board flow, because kanata has no firmware to replay them on.


## 2026-11-11 follow-up: build and flash never strand anyone

### D-497: Privileged and proof-recording steps act only on evidence that can't be faked or misread. Follows the review of #270 (D-486, D-493, D-495). (Claude, from CodeRabbit's review of #270)

- **The daemon's plist.** **Set up and start kanata** no longer has root copy the plist from `~/Library/Application Support/kanata`, which any process running as the person could swap while the password dialog was open. The script carries the plist base64-encoded; root decodes it into a file it creates with `mktemp` beside `/Library/LaunchDaemons/dev.keyloom.kanata.plist`, sets `root:wheel` 644, renames it into place, and only then boots the daemon out and bootstraps it. A failed step removes the temporary file and bootstraps nothing. The copy in keyloom's folder stays for the CLI's printed `sudo` command (D-231). Still open: the daemon runs Homebrew's kanata and reads `keyloom.kbd`, both writable by the person, as root. That is inherent to a Homebrew-installed root daemon, and 21's rule that keyloom never emits `cmd` or `danger-enable-cmd` is the guard on the config side.
- **Replace it** acts only while kanata still runs a config keyloom didn't write. Any other fresh reading, such as remapping switched off since the confirm opened, refuses with `kanata_not_foreign`; the confirm closes and a toast says what changed. The write runs under a claimed sync row (`claimSync`), so no sync here or in another keyloom process starts during it and a failed reload can't restore its own file over this one. A claim held elsewhere is `busy`. The replace is recorded as a manual sync.
- **The exit-chord proof** is recorded only when kanata's port refuses the connection (`ECONNREFUSED`). A port that accepts and never answers, or any other failed read, is `kanata_unreadable`; nothing is recorded, and the toast offers **Check again**. `kanata_still_running` offers the same button. The CLI's `keyloom remap confirm-exit-chord` is unchanged: it is the person's own statement (D-235).
- **Docker.** A `docker version` that fails with "permission denied" is `no-access`, not `daemon-down`: no start runs, Build firmware says "Give keyloom access to Docker first.", and the callout offers Check again. On Linux it shows `sudo usermod -aG docker $USER` with Copy and says a new login is needed (keyloom can't run sudo or log the person out); on a Mac it names the engine to quit and reopen. A daemon that only answers slowly is asked again, never restarted: a Colima restart stops the VM and whatever it is running. Only a daemon that says it is down (its socket refuses) is started, which is still the wedged-Colima case in docs/research/docker-engine-start.md. A daemon still slow at the deadline is left running, with "Check again in a minute". The launch command and the wait share one deadline: the launch gets what is left of it instead of a fixed 60 s, and Docker is asked once more after a launch that used the whole window. The install path keeps its own 15-minute Colima start.
- **After review:** `keyloom remap confirm-exit-chord` asks kanata first, through the same `probeRemapper` as the app's button, so the CLI can't record the proof while kanata runs. Only Docker's socket error is `no-access`; the "permission denied" an unreadable `~/.docker/config.json` prints still leaves a stopped daemon to be started. A start with only seconds left before the deadline isn't launched, so an engine is never killed half-started.
- **After the second review (#274):** **Replace it** finishes its claimed row on any failure after the claim, opening its log included, so a failed replace never leaves later syncs `busy`. It is the engine's running sync while it writes, so `stop()` waits for the apply and its recorded result. The exit-chord button reads the keyboards first and probes kanata last, and records the proof only when that reading shows kanata gone and the probe is refused. The Linux docker-group note says Docker warns the group grants root-level access. A Mac whose engine is Colima gets a command with Copy instead of "quit and reopen": Colima makes its socket readable by its owner only (`srw-------`, abiosoft/colima#1395), so a refused socket belongs to another account, usually root after `sudo colima start`. The command stops that copy, gives `~/.colima` back and starts Colima as the person. A cancelled build stops a running `colima start` too; a launch stopped that way is a failed start.
- **Stopping never cuts a write off.** `SyncEngine.stop()` read the step from the row as claimed, which is always `building`, so quitting keyloom or `serve --replace` during a flash aborted zapp or the DFU tool mid-write. It now reads the live step, as `setPaused` does, and waits for a write up to the grace period. The fake flasher now honours an abort while writing, so a test catches it.

### D-496: A pasted API key is kept in the macOS Keychain and used at once. Replaces "keyloom never stores your key" (19, 03) and answers Q21. (BK, 2026-11-11, asked "Keychain, Sign in with Codex, or memory only?", chose Keychain.)

"Chat needs a model" used to list terminal commands and end with "quit and reopen keyloom". That is a dead end for anyone who opened the desktop app: the fix was outside the app, and a restart was part of it.

- **One setup surface.** `ModelSetup` (`src/web/chat/ModelSetup.tsx`) serves "Chat needs a model", the picker's provider that needs setup, the review's **Set up a model**, and the account failures "The key was refused" (**Change the key**) and "Out of credits" (**Add credits**, **Use another key**). Each row is a password field with **Save and use it** and a link to the provider's key page, or **Sign in with Codex**.
- **Storage.** macOS: `security`, service `keyloom`, account = the provider. Linux: `secret-tool` with the same attributes, when it is installed. Windows, or Linux without it: no store, and the setup shows the environment variable to copy.
- **The key never goes into argv.** `security add-generic-password -w <key>` would put it in the process list. keyloom runs `security -q -i` and writes the `add-generic-password` line to its stdin; `secret-tool store` reads the secret from stdin. Checked against the real `security` with a throwaway item (service `keyloom-r1c-selftest`, created, read back with `find-generic-password -w`, deleted): the quoted line stores the value exactly, and `-U` updates it. A test runs the real runner against a stand-in `security` and checks its argv is `-q -i`. Pasted keys are limited to the characters provider keys use, so a paste can't break the quoted line.
- **Checked before it is saved.** One `models.retrieve` read (`src/app/credential-check.ts`, moved out of `doctor`), which spends no tokens. Auth or permission refusal: not saved, "Anthropic refused this key.", the field stays open. No network: saved, and the setup says the first message will tell.
- **Used at once.** `credentials().env()` fills an unset `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` from the store on every model build and status read, cached for 5 seconds, so a key saved by the app, or by the CLI next to a running server, answers the next message. No restart. Store commands run through the toolchain's `capture` with a 60-second limit, so a Keychain that never answers reads as empty instead of hanging chat.
- **The environment wins.** A key in the environment is shown as "from your environment" with its last four characters, and can't be saved over or forgotten from the app.
- **Where the key never goes.** The database, the logs, the event stream, error messages and API responses. Responses carry `{ source, last4 }` only; the save route answers a bad body with a fixed message, never the parser's view of the value.
- **Sign in with Codex.** `POST /api/chat/provider/codex-login` runs `codex login` (`src/app/codex-signin.ts`, through the toolchain's `run`), which opens the browser. The status says `waiting` (with OpenAI's sign-in URL when Codex prints it, for **Open the sign-in page again**) until it exits, and `useProviderStatus` asks again every 1.5 seconds until then, one request at a time. Cancel stops it; it gives up after 10 minutes. keyloom still never reads the ChatGPT credential (D-108). The button is hidden when `codex` is not installed; offering an install is not part of this round.
- **CLI.** `keyloom provider` shows each credential's source and last four characters. `keyloom provider key set <anthropic|openrouter>` reads the key from piped stdin (a terminal gets a usage error, so the key is never echoed) and `keyloom provider key forget <provider>` removes it.
- **Tests never touch a real Keychain.** The test preload sets `KEYLOOM_CREDENTIAL_STORE=none`; tests inject a store or a stand-in `security`. The CLI test checks a key against a local stand-in for Anthropic's models endpoint through `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`.
- **Known weakness.** An item created by `/usr/bin/security` trusts that binary, so any process of the same user can read it with `security find-generic-password -w` without a prompt. That is the same exposure as a key in a shell profile, and less than a key in the environment of every process. A signed helper with its own access list would close it; not this round.

### D-495: Refusals and failures carry codes the web app turns into the action that fixes them; the CLI keeps its own words. (Claude, in the dead-ends round, from BK: "I don't want dead ends in the app, or places where a user is just told that something is wrong without being offered a way out")

- **Sync refusals.** `SyncRefusal` (a `UsageError`, so CLI exit codes are unchanged) carries `archived`, `not_on_keyboard`, `cant_sync`, `not_connected`, `kanata_not_running`, `kanata_foreign`, `foreign_firmware`, `busy`, `unproven`, `keyboard_removed` or `kanata_still_running`; `/api/sync` answers 400 with that code. The toast maps it (`refusalNotice` in `src/web/sync/words.ts`) to Restore, Put it on…, Build and flash… or Replace it…, and never shows the server's sentence, which names CLI commands. Toasts gained an optional action button.
- **The sync pill.** "Put this layout on it" and `behind/other_config` open the put-on confirm (D-300) instead of a Sync now the engine refuses. A sync whose last failure was a build opens Build and flash, where Docker's callout and the log are; R1-A's Docker code can replace that test later. kanata running a config keyloom didn't write offers **Replace it…**, which applies the head with `overwriteForeign` through `POST /api/sync/replace-foreign`: a keyloom.kbd keyloom didn't write is kept as `keyloom.kbd.before-keyloom`, and a file kanata was started with elsewhere is untouched. Only the button does it; auto-apply still never replaces a foreign config (21).
- **The exit chord.** "I held it and kanata stopped" calls `POST /api/sync/confirm-exit-chord`, which records the proof only while kanata doesn't answer, so the button can't stand in for the chord (D-235 unchanged).
- **Typing lab.** A capture refused because the firmware can't stream carries `needs_sync`; the lab shows Sync now and Build and flash… beside it. A lab that fails to load says so with Try again. The MacBook keyboard no longer offers the lab (its remapper has no key stream).
- **Loads.** Layout reads give codes: `not_found` ("This layout is gone", with Show your other layouts and no Try again), `unreachable` and `token`. A layout keyloom can't draw offers Show your other layouts and Archive this config, and no longer asks to regenerate geometry. Version compare and a failed wall tile get Try again; a failed tile can still open its layout.
- **Unknown addresses.** A path that isn't an asset gets an HTML 404 with Open keyloom, carrying the session token only when the request had this session's own. `/api/` paths keep JSON.

### D-494: keyloom stopping is said once, app-wide, and the page recovers by itself. (Claude, in the dead-ends round)

- After about three seconds without the events stream, and only once the page has been connected, a slim banner says "keyloom stopped answering. Reconnecting…". In a browser it adds what to do in the app and in a terminal, with Copy for `keyloom serve`; inside the desktop app (Tauri's internals are on the page) it says only the app's line. When the stream returns the banner goes and the page reads its layouts again.
- A stream refused with 401 means keyloom restarted with a new link; the banner says so instead of reconnecting forever.
- Per-request failures no longer ask whether keyloom is "still running in your terminal", which was wrong in the desktop app.

### D-493: The MacBook remapper is set up from the app, through macOS's own password dialog. Replaces the "keyloom never installs it" part of D-231's cost line. (BK's brief for the dead-ends round)

- `GET /api/system/remapper?board=` returns the doctor's findings, each with a code. The sync popover, while kanata isn't running, shows them as steps with one button each: **Install kanata** (`brew install kanata`, one shared run, followed while it lasts), **Open Input Monitoring settings** and **Open Accessibility settings** (D-492), and **Set up and start kanata**.
- **Set up and start kanata** writes the plist into keyloom's folder (`setupRemapper`, which never replaces a keyloom.kbd it didn't write), then runs the install through `osascript … with administrator privileges`, whose prompt names what it does. It is the root command the CLI prints, without `sudo`, plus a bootout first so a daemon stopped by the exit chord starts again. keyloom never sees the password; it is the same trust as the printed `sudo` line, with no terminal. Every path is single-quoted for the shell and the whole command escaped for AppleScript; both are unit-tested, the shell side against a real `/bin/sh`.
- The steps re-read the machine after each action, and the pill says kanata is running only when the sync engine sees it answer. The driver has no installer keyloom can run, so its step links the pinned release page. The doctor now asks kanata on the port Apply uses (`KEYLOOM_KANATA_PORT`), not only the board's.
- The CLI is unchanged: `keyloom remap setup` still prints the command and runs nothing.

### D-492: keyloom opens System Settings at the right pane itself. (BK's brief for the dead-ends round)

- `POST /api/system/open-settings {pane}` takes a name from an allowlist (`input_monitoring`, `accessibility`, `files_and_folders`, `full_disk_access`, `login_items`), never a URL, and runs `open x-apple.systempreferences:…` on macOS only. Anything else is a 400 before the computer is asked. The UI shows these buttons only on macOS. R1-A's next round can use it for Files and Folders.

### D-491: A build failure says why as a code, and the page never shows a terminal command as the fix. Claude, in the blueprint; from BK's dead-ends request ("I don't want dead ends in the app… Like saying Docker is not installed without offering a way or a button to install it.")

- **Codes, not sentences.** A build that stops at Docker finishes with `errorCode: "docker_unavailable"` (stored in `builds.error_code`, migration 0019) as well as its sentence. The release dialog maps the code to the Docker callout, so it never parses the sentence, and `BuildFailedError.code` carries the same code to the sync engine for the pill. A flash refused because its tool is missing answers 409 `flash_tool_missing`.
- **Try again repeats what failed.** The release dialog remembers its last action: a build or flash that failed to start is sent again, and only a poll that gave up polls again (R1-10).
- **A bundled docker CLI is adopted, not linked.** When `docker` is missing from the PATH but an installed Docker Desktop, OrbStack or Rancher Desktop bundles one, keyloom puts that folder on its own PATH and carries on. This replaces D-487's `sudo ln -sf` line, which asked for an admin password to fix something keyloom already knew how to find.
- **Backticks stay in the terminal.** Server sentences keep their `code` spans for the CLI; the page strips them.

### D-490: A board keyloom can't flash gets its file, not a Flash button that always fails. Claude, in the blueprint.

Every build now carries a `flashPlan`: the board's manual steps when its recipe is `manual`, the tool a flash runs (`zapp`, `dfu-util`, `dfu-programmer`, `avrdude`) and whether it is installed, and whether the file can be shown in Finder. For a manual board the flash step shows the board's steps with **Download firmware** (`GET /api/builds/:id/artifact`) and, on macOS, **Show in Finder** (`POST /api/builds/:id/reveal`, which runs `open -R`). Only the file a successful build row names, inside the data directory's `artifacts/` folder, is served or revealed; a row edited to point elsewhere is a 404. The CLI keeps its sentence with the file path.

### D-489: Flash tools are checked before the flash, and installed with a button. Claude, in the blueprint.

The build's flash plan says whether Zapp (or the board's DFU tool) is on this computer before anyone clicks Flash. If it isn't, the flash step shows **Install Zapp**, which runs `brew install zapp` through the same install job as Docker, follows its output, and re-reads the build when it finishes, so the Flash button appears without another click. `zapp` is in homebrew-core (`brew info --json=v2 zapp`: tap `homebrew/core`, 1.0.2, checked 2026-11-11), so the command the code already printed was right. `startFlash` refuses before it creates a flash row (`FlashToolMissingError`), so a missing tool is an install to do, not a failed flash. Without Homebrew the button is **Get Zapp**, linking the tool's page.

### D-488: Install Docker is a button: Homebrew installs Colima, and keyloom starts it. Extends D-486 and D-487. BK's call, through the dead-ends brief.

On a Mac with Homebrew, **Install Docker** runs `brew install colima docker`, then starts Colima on the D-486 path (`startDockerEngine`, with Colima named as the engine because a fresh docker CLI points at no known socket) and waits for `docker version` to answer. Colima is headless, open source, needs no admin password and no app to click through, and keyloom already starts and restarts it. Docker Desktop's cask can ask for `sudo`, which a server can't answer, so it stays a link: the callout ends "Prefer an app? Docker Desktop · OrbStack". The install runs as one in-memory job per tool (`POST /api/system/install`, polled with `GET /api/system/install?tool=`), streams Homebrew's last lines in place, and ends in "Docker is installed and running" or in the failure with Homebrew's output and Try again. Without Homebrew, or on Linux and Windows, the primary button is **Download Docker Desktop** (or the engine docs page). While the dialog is open and Docker is missing, it asks again every four seconds, so a Docker installed some other way shows up without a click; Check again stays as a secondary button. **Build firmware** is disabled with "Install Docker first." beside it while there is no Docker.

- **No buildx.** keyloom's only `docker build` is the Glove80 image (`src/firmware/zmk/image.ts`), whose Dockerfile uses no BuildKit-only syntax. Without the buildx plugin the docker CLI falls back to the legacy builder with a deprecation warning, and errors only when `DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1` is set (docker/cli `cmd/docker/builder.go`), which keyloom never sets. So `docker-buildx` is not installed, and `~/.docker/config.json` is not touched. Revisit when the legacy builder is removed.
- **Safety.** No test runs Homebrew or opens Finder: every runner is injected, and the test preload sets `KEYLOOM_REFUSE_SYSTEM_ACTIONS=1`, which makes the real runner refuse.
- **Not done yet:** a CLI command for the install (D-030). `keyloom doctor` still prints the command.

## 2026-11-11 follow-up

### D-487: Start the engine the person actually runs, and say plainly when it is not one keyloom can start. Extends D-486. (BK: "what if a user doesn't have colima but is using another docker engine? what happens if a user installs and they don't have docker at all on their computer?")

D-486 asked the `docker` CLI for its context and knew four answers: Docker Desktop, OrbStack, Colima and systemd. Everything else was treated as "nothing to start", which is where "another engine" and "no Docker" got the same treatment.

- **The context decides.** `docker context inspect <name>` gives `Endpoints.docker.Host`, and that socket is the fingerprint: `~/.docker/run/docker.sock` and `desktop-linux` are Docker Desktop, `~/.orbstack/` is OrbStack, `~/.colima/<profile>/` is Colima, `~/.rd/` and `rancher-desktop` are Rancher Desktop, `podman.sock` is Podman, `~/.lima/<vm>/sock/docker.sock` is Lima. Each starts through its own launcher: `open -a <app>` for the apps, `podman machine start`, `limactl start <vm>`, `colima start` or `colima restart`, `systemctl start docker` or `systemctl --user start docker-desktop` on Linux.
- **A context on another machine is not started from here.** `ssh://`, `tcp://` (other than loopback), `http(s)://` and `npipe://` come back as `elsewhere`, and the sentence names the address. A local socket from a tool keyloom has never launched comes back as `unknown`, naming the context and socket. Neither is guessed at; an invented command is worse than a sentence.
- **No Docker at all is an install, not a start.** `dockerInstall(platform)` returns the command that works on this computer (`brew install --cask docker` on a Mac with Homebrew, `winget install --id Docker.DockerDesktop -e` on Windows, the distribution's page on Linux) and a lighter alternative. Build and flash shows the command and the link; `keyloom doctor` says the same sentence.
- **An engine whose CLI never reached the PATH is a link, not an install.** A GUI launch, or an app that was never told to install its command line tools, leaves `/Applications/Docker.app` in place with no `docker` on the PATH. `dockerStatus` answers `cli-missing` with the `sudo ln -sf … /usr/local/bin/docker` line instead of telling the person to install what they already have.
- **Verification.** Unit tests cover every signature and every sentence, and BK's Mac still resolves to Colima through `restart`. The `not-installed` callout with the Homebrew command and the remote-engine callout were driven through a real `keyloom serve` with fake `docker` and `brew` on the PATH (screenshots in [../research/docker-engine-start.md](../research/docker-engine-start.md#the-engines-keyloom-does-not-ship)). Docker Desktop, OrbStack, Rancher Desktop, Podman, Lima and the Linux services are matched by their documented sockets and started by their documented commands; none of them was launched on a machine that has them.

## 2026-11-10 follow-up

### D-486: keyloom starts the Docker engine itself, and says where it can't, instead of stopping at "Docker isn't running". (BK: "there could be a button here that the user can just click to resolve it, or better yet, since this is so integral to the functioning of the app, just start the daemon yourself… Do we really need docker? there's no other way to do it?")

Firmware is still built in Docker. The alternatives were re-examined and are worse: a host toolchain per family means three installs (arm-none-eabi-gcc and a pinned ZSA fork for QMK, west plus the Zephyr SDK and per-board modules for ZMK, a pinned hardware module for the Keebart board), several gigabytes on the person's machine, and version drift that breaks a build keyloom can't reproduce; a hosted build service contradicts local-first and the no-backend rule (D-001). Docker is the one prerequisite that keeps every board's build the same everywhere.

What changes is whose problem a closed engine is. A build no longer stops with a sentence telling the person to open Docker Desktop. `dockerStatus` now names the problem (`not-installed`, `daemon-down`, `slow`), `detectDockerEngine` finds what runs the daemon here, and `startDockerEngine` starts it and waits up to two minutes, reporting progress into the build log. It is on by default: starting an app the person already installed on their own machine is not a privileged act, and `KEYLOOM_DOCKER_AUTOSTART=0` turns it off. `keyloom build`, `keyloom emulate`, workload replay and the review all go through `ensureDocker`; `keyloom doctor --start-docker` starts it for the diagnostic path.

- **Engines:** Docker Desktop and OrbStack through `open -a`, chosen by the docker context the CLI points at; Colima through `colima start`, or `colima restart` when the VM is up and the daemon inside it is wedged (the case BK hit on 2026-11-10: `docker version` refused while `colima status` said running); `systemctl start docker` on Linux, with the `sudo` line in the failure.
- **The click, for when keyloom can't start it:** `GET /api/system/docker` and `POST /api/system/docker/start` (src/api/system.ts) back a line in Build and flash that says Docker isn't answering, offers to start it now, and, when nothing is installed, links the install instead.
- **Proof:** `keyloom doctor --start-docker` on BK's Mac found Colima, restarted the wedged VM in 56 s and reported `docker: running, server 28.4.0` with the toolchain behind it. Screenshots of the note and the missing-Docker callout at 1440 and 390 are in [../research/docker-engine-start.md](../research/docker-engine-start.md).
- **No test starts an engine:** `test/helpers/no-real-docker-start.ts` sets the opt-out for the suite, and the start paths are tested through injected `startEngine`/fake engines.

## 2026-11-04 follow-up

### D-482: A verified standalone flash updates the keyboard's recorded version; an unverified write is never reported as fully complete. (BK: “verification should be part of flashing”; “fix the app so that it reports the correct state”)

The flasher's completion includes its build-marker readback when the board supports it. A verified standalone flash records that exact revision in version history, just as a verified Sync does; offline status uses the newest verified revision from either path. A write that finishes without confirming the marker is shown as “Flashed, not verified,” does not become a version, and its log remains available. This replaces D-301/D-307's sync-only recording rule. Verification is of the running build marker, not a full key-by-key readback.

### D-483: Apple Silicon CI and release jobs run on BK's Mac, not hosted macOS runners. Replaces the `macos-15` runner in D-329. (2026-11-04, BK: "see how i configured things to use this mac as a runner for macos stuff and then please copy that so that on this app we do something similar instead of relying on github runners")

On 2026-11-04 PR #258's `desktop` and Apple Silicon `device-helper` jobs were cancelled twice after 20 to 25 minutes without starting a step: "The job was not acquired by Runner of type hosted even after multiple attempts", under GitHub's notice that macOS arm64 capacity is constrained. Releases need the same runners, so a release could publish without its Apple Silicon CLI or desktop app.

The jobs now run on a repository runner on BK's M3 Max, labelled `keyloom-builder` and set up the way mxr's `mxr-builder` is (one runner per repository under `~/.actions-runner/<repo>`, a launchd service running as BK). It takes release.yml's Apple Silicon `build-native` and `build-desktop`, the signing preflight's Apple Silicon entry, and ci.yml's Apple Silicon `device-helper` and `desktop`. Intel and Linux stay on hosted runners, as does Windows: the Mac can't run them natively, and their runners were not short.

Because the runner is BK's workstation:
- `.github/actions/self-hosted-rust-home` (adapted from mxr) gives jobs a rustup and Cargo home of their own in the runner's tool cache, ahead of BK's `~/.dotfiles/bin` cargo wrapper, and caps Cargo at five jobs. On mxr's runner, a job left on BK's `~/.cargo` and `~/.rustup` rewrote his toolchain.
- `build-desktop` adds its temporary signing keychain to BK's keychain search list and restores the list in an `always()` step. The hosted-runner version replaced the list with `login.keychain-db` alone. `scripts/sign-device-helper.sh` already restored it.
- No job reaches his keyboards. The Rust integration tests compile only with the synthetic features, the unit tests use synthetic records and PTYs, and the installed-CLI smokes run with a scratch `HOME` and no device discovery.
- Only BK's own work runs there (BK: "only run on this mac for work done by me, i dont want this to be a malicious way to run things on my mac"). The repository is public, and a pull request runs its own copy of the workflow files, so a fork can ask for any runner by label. ci.yml therefore runs on `push` and `workflow_dispatch`, no longer on `pull_request`; only people with write access, BK alone, can push or dispatch. Its Apple Silicon jobs ask for the Mac only when BK, or release.yml's dispatch on the release PR, started the run. The guarantee itself is the runner's job-started hook (`infra/keyloom-builder/job-started.sh`, installed outside the repository): it fails any job that is not a push or dispatch by BK, or a dispatch by `github-actions[bot]`, before any step runs. A fork's token can't dispatch workflows.

A fork's pull request now gets no CI run; its commits get one once BK pushes them. If the Mac is off or asleep, these jobs wait for it.

### D-484: The desktop release signs through Tauri's own keychain, kept out of BK's home. Replaces D-483's `build-desktop` keychain handling. (2026-11-04)

v0.72.2's `build-desktop`, the first on `keyloom-builder`, shipped no app. It imported the Developer ID certificate into a keychain of its own and added that to BK's search list. His login keychain holds the same identity, so Tauri's `codesign`, which names no keychain, found it twice and refused: `ambiguous (matches … in …/build.keychain-db and … in …/login.keychain-db)`. Hosted runners have an empty login keychain, so this never showed there. The release has its CLI assets, and the release workflow refuses to rebuild a release that already has assets, so the app ships in the next release.

`build-desktop` now passes the certificate to Tauri as `APPLE_CERTIFICATE`/`APPLE_CERTIFICATE_PASSWORD`. Tauri imports it into a keychain of its own, adds it to the existing search list, signs with `codesign --keychain` and deletes the keychain on exit (`tauri-macos-sign` `keychain.rs` @ `tauri-cli-v2.12.1`). `scripts/sign-device-helper.sh` already signs with `--keychain`, and signed the helper with the same identity on the same Mac in that run. Tauri puts that keychain in `$HOME/Library/Keychains` and the decoded `.p12` in `TMPDIR`, so the build step points both at scratch folders in `RUNNER_TEMP`. An `always()` step then deletes any keychain or certificate left there and restores BK's search list, in case Tauri dies before its own cleanup.

- **Options rejected:** a search list holding only the job's keychain during the build, which takes BK's login keychain off his own list for ten minutes; signing with the identity in his login keychain, which a launchd session may not be able to unlock without a prompt.

## 2026-11-03 follow-up

### D-481: Desktop updates are installed only after the user chooses to install and restart. (BK: “offer the user a prompt to restart and install”)

The updater still checks at launch and every six hours, but finding an update no longer downloads or installs it. keyloom posts a notification and enables a menu-bar action naming the version. Choosing it downloads and installs the signed update, then requests restart through the normal app exit path so the shell can stop its owned server. A failed install leaves the action available to retry. The updater remains compiled only when its signing keys are configured (D-328).

## 2026-11-02 follow-up

### D-485: The pegboard flight names the moving board on the live element before the transition starts, and on both ends. Extends D-478. (2026-11-02, BK: "the animation is not really working, the page just changes, i don't knowif we need to use view transitions or something I don't know"; numbered when recorded on 2026-11-04, after D-479 had gone to another decision)

- **Name it before the first snapshot:** `document.startViewTransition` takes its first snapshot synchronously. A `view-transition-name` set through React state is not in the DOM yet at that point, so the browser saw nothing to fly and crossfaded the page. The click handler sets `style.viewTransitionName = "flying-board"` on the live `.peg-tilt` element, and the name is cleared when the transition finishes (`src/web/home/Pegboard.tsx`, `src/web/App.tsx`, 01c50d1c).
- **Both ends carry the name:** a named group flies only when an element has that name in both snapshots. The desk's `.stage > .keyboard-well` always carries it (`src/web/shell/shell.css`), so opening pairs it with `.peg-tilt`; going home pairs it with the slot the board came from (`.peg-flying-cradle`).
- **The board flies, the ledge stays:** the name sits on `.peg-tilt` (the keyboard), not on `.peg-ledge`, so the bracket stays on the wall. The wall itself recedes (scale 0.93, up 14 px, fading out) under its own `pegboard-wall` name.
- **The browser computes the path:** the `flying-board` group sets only duration, easing, z-index and shadow. Keyframed transforms on the group would replace the movement the browser interpolates between the two boxes.

### D-476: Manage a keyboard collection separately from config archives, with a bounded picker and a contribution handoff. (2026-11-02, BK: "fix all this please")

Extends D-266, D-277, D-300, D-315 and D-336. The top bar uses one bounded, searchable keyboard picker at every width; a collection of 40 boards does not expand the header. Manage keyboards offers reversible removal, restore and board-only contribution export. Archived-only boards are recoverable under Removed and excluded from normal switching. Collection entries still identify board models; this does not claim physical-device-instance targeting from chapter 30.

Migration 0017 records removed board IDs without deleting or archiving their configs. Removal refuses an active sync or firmware operation, pauses sync, and excludes the keyboard from sync decisions and setup offers. Sync launch checks membership in the same transaction as claiming a run. Restore retains paused sync and restores one config for an old archived-only board; no firmware or remapper write is implied. The current-config archive guard remains intact because collection removal is a different action.

First-run setup exposes the existing detected-keyboard questions without requiring another layout. People can also choose a supported board or search QMK/ZMK without USB. A new board with no source configs can start a config or import its keymap. An unmodelled board keeps the firmware's key geometry and shared sculpted caps, with a generated shaded, raised enclosure; crafted display data wins when available.

`keyboard list|remove|restore` and authenticated collection routes back the UI. `keyboard contribute` exports board data plus a handoff, with an optional upstream ID and a new output directory; the app downloads the same data. Exports exclude personal configs and recordings and publish nothing. `/add-keyboard` owns photo-based refinement and the checked board-folder PR. A draft can be prepared without photos or hardware, but an owner must supply honest build/flash verification before hardware support is accepted.

## 2026-11-01 follow-up

These entries were numbered D-469/D-470 on the unmerged encoder branch. They are D-471/D-472 here to preserve the already-merged D-469 view-opening decision.

### D-471: Encoder turns belong to each layer, with ordinary editable presses. Replaces D-462's fixed-turn limit and the relevant part of D-105. (BK: fix the encoder request and investigate changing the build)

ZMK already dispatches `sensor-bindings` per active layer; repeating defaults was keyloom's generator policy, not a keyboard restriction. Optional `Layer.sensorBindings` and `setSensorBindings` now carry rotation pairs or whole imported calls. Null resets to board defaults; old documents retain their defaults. The Corne's four sensor slots map to press positions `[6, 6, 7, 7]`, so editing one knob changes its alternative footprints together. Presses still use `setBinding`.

The validator checks count, keycodes, understood native mouse actions, function loss and stale bases; an edit cannot silently drop the last reachable media control. Turn edits highlight the encoder press and appear in proposal/history details. The key card shares one draft and Apply with its press, and the CLI uses the same ops. Import preserves sensor calls and definitions instead of dropping them; parameterized opaque sensor calls block layer renumbering. Generated rotation helpers reimport conservatively as opaque calls; JSON exports preserve their typed pairs.

Mouse turns need dedicated timing: the pinned ZMK's 5 ms sensor tap ends before its 16 ms mouse tick. Generated constant-speed input devices use 5 ms ticks and 20 ms taps, leaving ordinary mouse keys unchanged; scroll uses a whole-unit-per-tick speed. Headers, listeners and pointing Kconfig are generated, with no hardware-module fork. Generation/import, adversarial validation, scripted-agent apply and a compiled scratch-CLI proposal/apply/generate journey passed. The first firmware build stopped because Docker was not running. On BK's retry, build `bld_01M6ACXF8GWXY7Z9DYMP59X0H8` compiled both Keebart halves and produced verified single-image UF2s (family `0xada52840`); the final devicetree keeps different sensor tables on Base and Numbers, and pointing/input-two-axis/sensor-rotate support is enabled. Physical pointer movement and a hardware flash are not claimed.

### D-472: A completed reply leads with the result; lookup failures stay explicit without expanding successful working. Supersedes D-412's ordering; refines D-398, D-409 and D-456. (BK: fix the screenshot's failures, hierarchy and dead end)

While streaming, observed work leads. At rest the answer and saved proposals lead, then working details. A separate failure-count disclosure keeps failed steps explicit, with allowlisted error codes translated into a reason and next action. Raw exception messages and tool inputs never enter presentation. Unknown, declined and partial outcomes remain outside routine success. A failed step no longer forces successful steps and working notes open; incomplete-history warnings remain visible.

The agent corrects and retries invalid calls rather than treating them as evidence of hardware limits. Function lookup accepts canonical codes, firmware aliases and common media labels, and includes encoder turns. If still blocked, its reply must say what remains unchanged and give a concrete next action or useful checked partial proposal. This prompt guidance is not a deterministic guarantee of model compliance.

The active editing context appears once, rather than below every current-thread user message. Known costs retain the dollar footer; token-only accounting is behind Usage, and wholly unreported cost/usage produces no footer. No success status is inferred from a finished reply or from recovered tool calls.

## The arc, briefly

1. BK pitched the idea on 2026-09-28. He's a keyboard enthusiast who owns several ergonomic boards. He wants "one configurator to rule them all" for QMK and ZMK, with a chat agent he can describe his habits and layout to, which "is able to just go rip and build the thing for me" and suggest improvements.
2. The assistant researched the field, found two nearby projects (CustomKeymap and keymap-ai), and raised two doubts: whether the idea was a business, and the cost of promising "all keyboards".
3. **BK reset both.** It's an open-source project, not a business. He supports only his own keyboards, and anyone else adds theirs through an extension system, "maybe as a skill".
4. BK named the real pain. He tolerates an annoying layout because diagnosing and reconfiguring is tedious. He wants to describe a frustration and get a recommendation, and to do something once and say "do the same thing on these other areas".
5. Two concrete problems followed. Option–Option is impossible with Option as a home row mod on S. A Command–Shift key has nowhere to go when every key is used. The assistant worked through the remedies, rejected double-tap S, and shaped "make room" proposals with alternatives and trade-offs.
6. BK added memory: keyloom should remember why something was placed, even after he's forgotten.
7. On 2026-10-11 BK asked whether this could be built. The assistant said yes and named the hard parts: accurate import, QMK/ZMK translation, validating rearrangements, and reliable build and flash. Solving BK's two problems on his own boards would prove it.
8. In the blueprint session (2026-10-15), BK chose the name and location, the two target boards, and a local web app plus CLI. Research found that the Moonlander has two revisions and only ZSA's fork builds the newer one. That set the QMK pipeline.
9. Mid-session, BK pointed to `nickcoutsos/keymap-editor` as "a pretty good editor" to learn from (D-044).

## Decisions

### D-001: One configurator for both QMK and ZMK. (BK)
- **Principle (BK):** "in the new agentic era, there's an opportunity to make one configurator to rule them all. So just one configurator that supports both QMK and ZMK"

### D-002: The firmware follows from the chosen keyboard. (BK)
- BK: it "is able to detect which it is based on the keyboard we're talking about … So I'll just select what keyboard I'm talking about."
- **How:** the board definition names its firmware ([04](04-boards-and-contributions.md)).

### D-003: Keep a visual key-by-key editor alongside the chat. (BK)
- BK: "instead of just having, like, a configurator that shows you the keys and you do the thing one by one—you can do this, of course—but it also has, like, a bit of a chat interface".

### D-004: A chat agent builds the layout BK describes. (BK)
- BK: "after I explain all of it, the agent is able to just go rip and build the thing for me."

### D-005: The agent suggests layout changes from BK's habits. (BK)
- BK: "I can even ask for suggestions … this is the key that I use most because I use this program and whatever, right? And it's able to, like, figure it out and suggest some layout things for me."

### D-006: The layout vocabulary covers BK's real layout. (BK)
- BK's examples: QWERTY on the base layer; a number layer with "the numbers are on the home row"; "the symbols are on the number layer, but on the row just above the home row"; "I've got home row modes on layer number one on the home row, and I'm using, you know, J and F, you know, for command, so when I hold, it's command" (the transcript's "modes" is "mods").
- These are v1's minimum vocabulary and eval scenarios ([11](11-observability-and-evals.md)).

### D-007: Open source, not a business. (BK; replaces the assistant's business framing)
- **Earlier framing:** the assistant said the business case "still needs evidence".
- **BK:** "I don't plan for this to be a business, just an open-source project that hopefully other people would be interested in."
- **Consequence:** no accounts, billing or hosting (D-028).

### D-008: BK supports his own boards; others add theirs. (BK; replaces "all keyboards")
- **Earlier framing:** the assistant warned that "all keyboards" was "a much larger commitment".
- **BK:** "I also don't plan to create the support for all keyboards myself. I can just focus on my keyboards, and anyone else who wants another keyboard can come in and add the support themselves."

### D-009: A board is a definition plus an optional skill, with no marketplace. (BK proposed; agreed)
- **BK:** "if we build in some sort of, I don't know, plugin system or some sort of extension system that allows people to bring in their keyboards, maybe as a skill or something like that."
- **Refinement:** the assistant split board facts (physical keys, firmware target, build and flash) from agent guidance (quirks and conventions). The definition is something the app can validate; the skill helps the agent decide well.
- **Option rejected:** "an elaborate plugin marketplace". A folder per board in the repo is enough.

### D-010: Start from the frustration, not the solution. (BK)
- **Principle (BK):** "It would be great if I can just go somewhere, explain what my challenge is, and then get a recommendation of what to do. Not for me to have to figure it out and then configure it myself."
- **Why it matters:** BK has kept known annoyances "because the whole process of reconfiguring is a little— it's tedious."

### D-011: Bulk and pattern edits. (BK)
- BK: "It would be nice if we can just explain, and it does the thing on multiple keys, or maybe I do it once and say, okay, do the same thing on these other areas".
- **How:** expanded into plain per-slot ops, with no pattern op type ([03](03-agent.md#do-that-here-too-is-expanded-into-plain-ops)).

### D-012: A selection on the keyboard is context for the chat. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "Selecting five keys and saying 'give these the same hold behaviour' could be much nicer than describing five positions in words."

### D-013: Chat and the visual editor edit one layout. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- Proposed on 2026-09-28 and restated on 2026-10-11: "Both chat and manual editing would operate on the same layout."

### D-014: The agent proposes structured change sets; the app validates, previews and applies them. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "I'd have the agent propose structured changes that the app can validate, preview, and undo."
- **Option rejected (blueprint):** letting the model edit `keymap.c` or `.keymap` text. Nothing could check it before a flash.

### D-015: Every change carries an explanation and undoes as a unit. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "Every change should have a visible explanation and an undo." Also: "apply the entire rearrangement together … and let you undo the change as a whole."

### D-016: Importing the existing layout accurately comes first. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "That makes importing and understanding your existing layout central to the experience." It's the first hard part named on 2026-10-11, "including custom behaviours".

### D-017: Tap-hold feel is a first-class concern. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "The difficult part is getting the feel right … deciding whether a fast sequence was typing or an intentional shortcut is subtler."

### D-018: Recommendations are hypotheses tested through use. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "Recommendations would be hypotheses you test through use." And: "whether a position feels comfortable needs your feedback."

### D-019: Option–Option remedies; double-tap S rejected.
- **BK's problem:** "some softwares have a shortcut of Option-Option. I can't do Option-Option because my Option is holding down the S key."
- **Remedies:** (a) plain Option on another layer; (b) a macro on a key or combo; (c) change the app's shortcut.
- **Option rejected:** a double tap of S, "because you still need to type words like 'class.'" The validator warns on it ([07](07-change-validation.md#warnings)).

### D-020: "Make room": alternatives, displacement and reachability. (BK's problem; shape Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- **BK:** "I press Command Shift a lot … where the hell do I put it on this keyboard, right? … often all of my keys are used, and sometimes I have to replace something or move something else so that I make way for this more important thing".
- **Shape:** two or three concrete alternatives, affected keys highlighted, trade-offs explained, applied together, displaced functions kept reachable, one undo. **Replaced in part by D-452 (2026-10-28):** one best proposal first, refined by questions; displacement, reachability and one undo stand.

### D-021: BK's priorities are captured explicitly. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "Your keymap alone won't tell the agent which assignments you value." Examples: "I barely use that", "keep Backspace where it is", "I'm willing to relearn these two keys".

### D-022: Remember decisions and their reasons. (BK)
- **BK:** "over time, it can remember all of these conversations that we have and all of these preferences. So if later I want to come back and replace something that I previously said was important, it will know that this thing is important because of this reason, even if I have forgotten that reason."

### D-023: The shape of a remembered decision. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- **Fields:** goal or frustration; what was chosen and why; scope (keyboard, apps, habits); whether it was tried and helped.
- "'we thought this would be comfortable' and 'you used this for a month and liked it' should carry different weight."
- Memory can be inspected and corrected, links back to the conversation, and "an old decision should prompt a helpful reminder rather than prevent a new one."

### D-024: Firmware differences stay visible; there's no promise of exact translation. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "Firmware-specific features would stay visible where necessary; we shouldn't promise that every behaviour translates perfectly between firmware families."
- **How:** `native` bindings and `Passthrough` blocks ([02](02-layout-model.md)).

### D-025: The proof is BK's two problems solved on his own boards. (Claude proposed; unchallenged)
- "The convincing proof would be solving your two actual problems: making Option–Option accessible and finding a comfortable home for Command–Shift, with the resulting layouts working on your keyboards." Earlier: "get it working on two keyboards you own with different firmware."

### D-026: The name is keyloom, under planetaryescape. (BK, 2026-10-15)
- The repo is `planetaryescape/keyloom` at `~/code/planetaryescape/keyloom`. planetaryescape is where BK's published products go, and this one is meant for others.
- **Options rejected:** the name "layerwise"; placement under `bhekanik/`.

### D-027: The target boards are the Moonlander (QMK) and the Glove80 (ZMK). (BK, 2026-10-15)
- The transcript's "Clave AT" is the Glove80. BK confirmed it on 2026-10-15.
- **Options rejected:** a Corne on ZMK or QMK as the second board. The Corne waits until after the car (Q10).
- The Microsoft Sculpt isn't a target. BK named it as where his journey started. He has never owned a Dygma.

### D-028: A local web app plus a CLI, with no hosted backend. (BK, 2026-10-15)
- A bun server on localhost serves the editor and the agent, and a CLI drives the same core.
- **Options rejected:** (a) a Tauri desktop app, which adds native packaging on every rung for USB access that Zapp and UF2 drives already give; (b) a hosted web app like Oryx, which needs accounts, hosting and a processor contract for other people's layouts and chats.

### D-029: TypeScript, strict, ESM, on bun. (BK's standing rule for new projects)

### D-030: The CLI is the canonical surface. (Taken from spotuify and ms-todo.)
- Anything the web app does, the CLI does. Agents verify their work through it. D-028's "the CLI drives the same core" is the decision's root.

### D-031: The model is Claude, through `@anthropic-ai/sdk`, on the user's own key. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- `claude-opus-5-5`, adaptive thinking, effort `medium`, Tool Runner with strict zod tools.
- **Why:** BK's tools already use Claude, and the SDK's zod tool helpers match keyloom's zod schemas.
- **Revisit:** Q3 (other providers for other users).
- **Replaced in part by D-106** (2026-10-15): the provider is now Anthropic, OpenRouter or Codex. Claude through OpenRouter keeps this entry's model and SDK.

### D-032: Local SQLite, append-only revisions. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Option rejected:** layouts as files in a git repo. Diffable, but it adds a second history beside revisions, and conversations and decisions don't fit it. `keyloom layout export` covers sharing. Revisit: Q7.

### D-033: keyloom has its own firmware-neutral layout model. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Options rejected:** (a) storing QMK C or ZMK devicetree as the truth, which the agent can't edit safely and which differs per firmware; (b) QMK's `keymap.json` as the model, which can't express combos or tap dance and isn't ZMK.

### D-034: Releases are compiled binaries on GitHub, through release-please and `install.sh`. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- `bun build --compile` embeds the web app (oven-sh/bun `a9c57bd` `docs/bundler/executables.mdx:557-640`).
- **Option rejected:** an npm package. The `keyloom` name is taken there by an unrelated password generator (v1.0.4, checked 2026-10-15). Q2.

### D-035: No keystroke logging in v1. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- Habits come from what BK says (D-005). The chat never discussed recording typing. Deferred, not rejected (Q5).

### D-036: The Glove80 builds on MoErgo's fork at a pinned tag, through its template's Docker route. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Options rejected:**
  - MoErgo's cloud Layout Editor builds: needs an account, has unpublished limits, and isn't local.
  - GitHub Actions in a config repo: a push per change and minutes per round trip.
  - Upstream ZMK: has the board, but MoErgo supports only its fork.
  - `main` instead of a tag: unpinned.
- **Source:** [../research/zmk-and-glove80.md](../research/zmk-and-glove80.md).

### D-037: The Glove80 import reads the Layout Editor's `.keymap` export. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Option rejected:** the Layout Editor's JSON export. MoErgo says "the JSON format may change" and import isn't guaranteed.

### D-038: The Glove80 flashes by copying the combined UF2 to each half's boot drive, right half first. (Claude, in the blueprint; taken from MoErgo's guide)

### D-039: Runtime updates (ZMK Studio, VIA, Vial) are deferred. v1 always reflashes. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Why:** Studio can't change hold-tap properties, macros or combos. It ignores later `.keymap` changes once it manages a keymap. Stock MoErgo builds probably lack it. VIA and Vial support only Moonlander rev A, and ZSA doesn't support them.
- **Revisit:** after the car. Instant binding swaps would make trying changes cheaper.
- **Replaced by D-096** (2026-10-15): live writes become an optimisation of Apply, built only if auto-sync proves too slow in use.

### D-040: The Moonlander builds from ZSA's QMK fork (`firmware25`), pinned. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Option rejected:** mainline QMK. It has only rev A, and the rev B PR closed unmerged on 2026-06-22. BK's board may be rev B (S1).
- **Cost:** no Speculative Hold, which `firmware25` lacks.
- **Source:** [../research/qmk-and-moonlander.md](../research/qmk-and-moonlander.md).

### D-041: The Moonlander import reads Oryx's source zip, parsed with tree-sitter C. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Options rejected:**
  - Oryx GraphQL: undocumented, introspection off, private layouts return 401. Deferred as a later convenience (S4).
  - `qmk c2json`: reads only the keymaps array, drops macro and tap-dance bodies, and needs Python.

### D-042: keyloom generates QMK C (`keymap.c`, `config.h`, `rules.mk`), not `keymap.json`. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Why:** JSON can't define combos or tap dance. Oryx's export is C. A JSON-plus-C hybrid splits one layout over two files.

### D-043: The Moonlander flashes through ZSA's Zapp CLI. (Claude, in the blueprint)
- **Options rejected:** Keymapp (a GUI); `wally-cli` (archived); `qmk flash` or `dfu-util` (rev B needs ZSA's bootloader IDs and offset, which Zapp handles).

### D-044: keymap-editor is the reference editor; take its interaction patterns and geometry math, not its architecture. (BK pointed to it; verdicts Claude, in the blueprint)
- **BK (2026-10-15):** "see what you can learn from https://github.com/nickcoutsos/keymap-editor it's a pretty good editor".
- **Finding:** the public source is a 2023 snapshot (`main` at `e7946792`, MIT). The deployed app BK has used is closed, and its author has stopped publishing source. The code reading and every verdict are in [../research/keymap-editor.md](../research/keymap-editor.md).
- **Adopted:**
  - rotated-key geometry math
  - the keyboard-first fuzzy picker
  - modifier toggles in the picker
  - keeping parameters when the kind changes
  - quick assign by typing
  - drag-select
  - layer duplicate and reorder
  - tree-sitter devicetree parsing
